Well... I suppose what finally pushed me to come in was that I lost my temper in a way that really surprised me. Raised my voice, got sharp with him. That’s not how I usually speak to people, especially young folks who are just trying to help.

Yes… they do. I mean, I don’t fall apart every time, but it upsets me more than I’d like to admit. It’s the way it comes out of nowhere. Yes… yes, there was. It started after I was robbed outside the bank. That was about a week ago now, though it feels a little strange saying “only a week,” because it keeps replaying enough that it feels longer in my head.

That was about a week ago now, though it feels a little strange saying “only a week,” because it keeps replaying enough that it feels longer in my head.\n\nBefore that happened, I wasn’t having these repeating thoughts like this, and I wasn’t getting so worked up. But ever since then — since that incident outside the bank — it’s been going on. I keep thinking, well, maybe in another week it’ll fade, but right now it’s still very much there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities since the robbery outside the bank?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have been, though I didn’t quite think of it that way at first. I’ve been taking the long way around now so I don’t have to pass the bank. It’s silly, maybe, because it’s just a building, and I know that, but when I come near it I can feel myself wanting to steer away before I even really decide to.\n\nAnd there’s the festival storage room, too. I haven’t gone in there since. There are things in there I probably should check on, but I keep putting it off because it brings the whole thing too close again. Just being around that sort of donation stuff… it makes me feel like I’m right back there, and I’d rather not put myself through it if I can avoid it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been having trouble sleeping since the robbery?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I have. Not terribly in the sense that I’m up pacing all night, but it’s definitely changed since the robbery.

I’ll get into bed feeling tired — really tired, like my body wants to sleep — and then once the house is quiet, I just sort of lie there listening. Every little noise catches my attention. A creak, a car going by, something settling in the walls… I find myself straining to hear it, as if I’m waiting for something, though I know I’m at home and nothing is happening.

And then when I do sleep, it doesn’t feel very deep. I’ve been waking up before dawn, earlier than I need to, and I’ll just be lying there thinking, “Well, that’s it then, I suppose I’m awake.” It leaves me feeling like I hardly slept, even if technically I was in bed for hours.

I haven’t gone in there since. There are things in there I probably should check on, but I keep putting it off because it brings the whole thing too close again. Just being around that sort of donation stuff… it makes me feel like I’m right back there, and I’d rather not put myself through it if I can avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the robbery?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. Not terribly in the sense that I’m up pacing all night, but it’s definitely changed since the robbery.\n\nI’ll get into bed feeling tired — really tired, like my body wants to sleep — and then once the house is quiet, I just sort of lie there listening. Every little noise catches my attention. A creak, a car going by, something settling in the walls… I find myself straining to hear it, as if I’m waiting for something, though I know I’m at home and nothing is happening.\n\nAnd then when I do sleep, it doesn’t feel very deep. I’ve been waking up before dawn, earlier than I need to, and I’ll just be lying there thinking, “Well, that’s it then, I suppose I’m awake.” It leaves me feeling like I hardly slept, even if technically I was in bed for hours.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're in a safe environment?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 79 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d say “constantly,” no. I mean, I understand what you’re asking, but I don’t know that I’m walking around all day scanning for danger exactly.

It’s more… I’m aware I don’t feel quite like myself, but if you mean am I always looking out the window or checking every corner or expecting someone to come at me, I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way. Maybe I’d have to think about it more, because sometimes you don’t realize you’re doing something until someone points it out. But sitting here now, I can’t honestly say yes, that’s definitely happening.

It was such a small thing, and I made it into something bigger. It bothered me enough that I thought maybe I ought to talk to someone before I do it again.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any recurring thoughts or feelings that you feel compelled to repeat or act on?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… not in the way you’re saying, I don’t believe. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I have been avoiding certain reminders, though not so much the news reports specifically. It’s more the places and the things connected to it.

I’ve been taking the long way around so I don’t have to pass the bank. I tell myself it’s just more convenient or that I wanted a bit of a walk, but if I’m honest, it’s because going past there makes it feel too close again. Even seeing the front of the building out of the corner of my eye, I feel myself wanting to turn away.

And I still haven’t gone into the festival storage room. There are things in there I probably ought to look at, but I keep putting it off. It’s tied up with the whole thing in my mind now, and I don’t like how it brings me right back to it. So yes, I suppose I’ve been avoiding reminders, just maybe in a quieter way than refusing to watch the news or anything like that.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well... I suppose what finally pushed me to come in was that I lost my temper in a way that really surprised me. I was helping out with some of the younger volunteers, and there was this tablecloth that was crooked — just a tablecloth, nothing important really — and I snapped at one of them over it. Raised my voice, got sharp with him. That’s not how I usually speak to people, especially young folks who are just trying to help.\n\nAfterward I kept thinking, why did I do that? It was such a small thing, and I made it into something bigger. It bothered me enough that I thought maybe I ought to talk to someone before I do it again.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any recurring thoughts or feelings that you feel compelled to repeat or act on?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… not in the way you’re saying, I don’t believe. I mean, I do go back over things in my head sometimes, probably more than I should — like what I said, whether I handled something badly — but I wouldn’t say I feel forced to repeat anything or do some particular action to make it stop.\n\nI’m not washing my hands over and over or checking locks a dozen times, if that’s what you mean. It’s more just… I get bothered by something and then I think about it too much afterward. But compelled to act on it? No, I haven’t noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s probably the thing that’s been bothering me most, if I’m being honest. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it. I’ll be doing something ordinary — sitting at my kitchen table, maybe trying to have my coffee or sort through the mail — and then out of nowhere I hear that man’s rough voice in my head again. Clear as anything, like he’s right there.\n\nOr I’ll suddenly see that donation envelope hitting the ground. Just that moment, over and over, even when I’m trying to think about something else. It’s strange because nothing is happening in the room, it’s quiet, and still my mind just pulls me right back to it. I try to shake it off, tell myself to stop going over it, but it comes in anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do these repeating thoughts cause you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… they do. I mean, I don’t fall apart every time, but it upsets me more than I’d like to admit. It’s the way it comes out of nowhere. I can be sitting at my kitchen table, just minding my own business, and suddenly I hear that man’s rough voice again in my head, or I see that donation envelope hitting the ground like it’s happening right in front of me.\n\nAnd it’s not even that I’m trying to remember it. That’s what bothers me. If I sat down and chose to think about it, maybe that would be one thing, but this just sort of pushes its way in. Then I feel tense and unsettled, and it takes me a while to get back to whatever I was doing. It makes a quiet moment not feel quiet anymore, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did something particularly stressful or traumatic happen to you before these repeating thoughts and loss of temper started?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, there was. It started after I was robbed outside the bank. That was about a week ago now, though it feels a little strange saying “only a week,” because it keeps replaying enough that it feels longer in my head.\n\nBefore that happened, I wasn’t having these repeating thoughts like this, and I wasn’t getting so worked up. But ever since then — since that incident outside the bank — it’s been going on. I keep thinking, well, maybe in another week it’ll fade, but right now it’s still very much there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities since the robbery outside the bank?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have been, though I didn’t quite think of it that way at first. I’ve been taking the long way around now so I don’t have to pass the bank. It’s silly, maybe, because it’s just a building, and I know that, but when I come near it I can feel myself wanting to steer away before I even really decide to.\n\nAnd there’s the festival storage room, too. I haven’t gone in there since. There are things in there I probably should check on, but I keep putting it off because it brings the whole thing too close again. Just being around that sort of donation stuff… it makes me feel like I’m right back there, and I’d rather not put myself through it if I can avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the robbery?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. Not terribly in the sense that I’m up pacing all night, but it’s definitely changed since the robbery.\n\nI’ll get into bed feeling tired — really tired, like my body wants to sleep — and then once the house is quiet, I just sort of lie there listening. Every little noise catches my attention. A creak, a car going by, something settling in the walls… I find myself straining to hear it, as if I’m waiting for something, though I know I’m at home and nothing is happening.\n\nAnd then when I do sleep, it doesn’t feel very deep. I’ve been waking up before dawn, earlier than I need to, and I’ll just be lying there thinking, “Well, that’s it then, I suppose I’m awake.” It leaves me feeling like I hardly slept, even if technically I was in bed for hours.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're in a safe environment?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “constantly,” no. I mean, I understand what you’re asking, but I don’t know that I’m walking around all day scanning for danger exactly.\n\nIt’s more… I’m aware I don’t feel quite like myself, but if you mean am I always looking out the window or checking every corner or expecting someone to come at me, I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way. Maybe I’d have to think about it more, because sometimes you don’t realize you’re doing something until someone points it out. But sitting here now, I can’t honestly say yes, that’s definitely happening.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific reminders of the robbery, such as news reports or conversations about it, that you've been trying to avoid?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have been avoiding certain reminders, though not so much the news reports specifically. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. It’s been about a week now, I’d say. Since the robbery outside the bank.

It started more or less right after that happened — not weeks before or anything like that. At first I thought maybe it was just the shock of it, you know, the first day or two being rattled, but it hasn’t quite lifted the way I expected. So… yes, about a week.

Raised my voice, got sharp with him. That’s not how I usually speak to people, especially young folks who are just trying to help.\n\nAfterward I kept thinking, why did I do that? It was such a small thing, and I made it into something bigger. It bothered me enough that I thought maybe I ought to talk to someone before I do it again.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any recurring thoughts or feelings that you feel compelled to repeat or act on?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… not in the way you’re saying, I don’t believe. I mean, I do go back over things in my head sometimes, probably more than I should — like what I said, whether I handled something badly — but I wouldn’t say I feel forced to repeat anything or do some particular action to make it stop.\n\nI’m not washing my hands over and over or checking locks a dozen times, if that’s what you mean. It’s more just… I get bothered by something and then I think about it too much afterward. But compelled to act on it? No, I haven’t noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s probably the thing that’s been bothering me most, if I’m being honest. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it. I’ll be doing something ordinary — sitting at my kitchen table, maybe trying to have my coffee or sort through the mail — and then out of nowhere I hear that man’s rough voice in my head again. Clear as anything, like he’s right there.\n\nOr I’ll suddenly see that donation envelope hitting the ground. Just that moment, over and over, even when I’m trying to think about something else. It’s strange because nothing is happening in the room, it’s quiet, and still my mind just pulls me right back to it. I try to shake it off, tell myself to stop going over it, but it comes in anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do these repeating thoughts cause you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… they do. I mean, I don’t fall apart every time, but it upsets me more than I’d like to admit. It’s the way it comes out of nowhere. I can be sitting at my kitchen table, just minding my own business, and suddenly I hear that man’s rough voice again in my head, or I see that donation envelope hitting the ground like it’s happening right in front of me.\n\nAnd it’s not even that I’m trying to remember it. That’s what bothers me. If I sat down and chose to think about it, maybe that would be one thing, but this just sort of pushes its way in. Then I feel tense and unsettled, and it takes me a while to get back to whatever I was doing. It makes a quiet moment not feel quiet anymore, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did something particularly stressful or traumatic happen to you before these repeating thoughts and loss of temper started?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, there was. It started after I was robbed outside the bank. That was about a week ago now, though it feels a little strange saying “only a week,” because it keeps replaying enough that it feels longer in my head.\n\nBefore that happened, I wasn’t having these repeating thoughts like this, and I wasn’t getting so worked up. But ever since then — since that incident outside the bank — it’s been going on. I keep thinking, well, maybe in another week it’ll fade, but right now it’s still very much there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities since the robbery outside the bank?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have been, though I didn’t quite think of it that way at first. I’ve been taking the long way around now so I don’t have to pass the bank. It’s silly, maybe, because it’s just a building, and I know that, but when I come near it I can feel myself wanting to steer away before I even really decide to.\n\nAnd there’s the festival storage room, too. I haven’t gone in there since. There are things in there I probably should check on, but I keep putting it off because it brings the whole thing too close again. Just being around that sort of donation stuff… it makes me feel like I’m right back there, and I’d rather not put myself through it if I can avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the robbery?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. Not terribly in the sense that I’m up pacing all night, but it’s definitely changed since the robbery.\n\nI’ll get into bed feeling tired — really tired, like my body wants to sleep — and then once the house is quiet, I just sort of lie there listening. Every little noise catches my attention. A creak, a car going by, something settling in the walls… I find myself straining to hear it, as if I’m waiting for something, though I know I’m at home and nothing is happening.\n\nAnd then when I do sleep, it doesn’t feel very deep. I’ve been waking up before dawn, earlier than I need to, and I’ll just be lying there thinking, “Well, that’s it then, I suppose I’m awake.” It leaves me feeling like I hardly slept, even if technically I was in bed for hours.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're in a safe environment?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “constantly,” no. I mean, I understand what you’re asking, but I don’t know that I’m walking around all day scanning for danger exactly.\n\nIt’s more… I’m aware I don’t feel quite like myself, but if you mean am I always looking out the window or checking every corner or expecting someone to come at me, I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way. Maybe I’d have to think about it more, because sometimes you don’t realize you’re doing something until someone points it out. But sitting here now, I can’t honestly say yes, that’s definitely happening.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific reminders of the robbery, such as news reports or conversations about it, that you've been trying to avoid?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have been avoiding certain reminders, though not so much the news reports specifically. It’s more the places and the things connected to it.\n\nI’ve been taking the long way around so I don’t have to pass the bank. Even seeing the front of the building out of the corner of my eye, I feel myself wanting to turn away.\n\nAnd I still haven’t gone into the festival storage room. So yes, I suppose I’ve been avoiding reminders, just maybe in a quieter way than refusing to watch the news or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these repeating thoughts and feelings of distress after the robbery?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a week now, I’d say. So… yes, about a week.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say your avoidance of certain places or activities is interfering with your daily life or routine?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 79 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I haven't been able to keep up with organizing the Día de los Muertos event the way I usually do, and I've stopped going to card nights because I don't feel steady around people right now.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think it is, more than I wanted to admit at first.

I usually help keep things moving for the Día de los Muertos event — you know, organizing, checking on what still needs doing, making sure people have what they need. It’s something I’ve done for a long time, and normally I’m pretty dependable with it. But lately I just haven’t been keeping up the way I usually do. I put things off, or I avoid going near certain parts of it, and then the day goes by and I realize I didn’t handle what I said I would.

And I’ve stopped going to card nights too. That’s not like me either. It’s not that I don’t care about the people there — I do. But right now I don’t feel steady around people, if that makes sense. I feel like I’d rather stay home than sit there trying to act normal. So yes, I’d say it’s starting to get in the way of my routine. Not in some dramatic way, maybe, but enough that I notice I’m not doing the things I normally do.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well... I suppose what finally pushed me to come in was that I lost my temper in a way that really surprised me. I was helping out with some of the younger volunteers, and there was this tablecloth that was crooked — just a tablecloth, nothing important really — and I snapped at one of them over it. Raised my voice, got sharp with him. That’s not how I usually speak to people, especially young folks who are just trying to help.\n\nAfterward I kept thinking, why did I do that? It was such a small thing, and I made it into something bigger. It bothered me enough that I thought maybe I ought to talk to someone before I do it again.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any recurring thoughts or feelings that you feel compelled to repeat or act on?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… not in the way you’re saying, I don’t believe. I mean, I do go back over things in my head sometimes, probably more than I should — like what I said, whether I handled something badly — but I wouldn’t say I feel forced to repeat anything or do some particular action to make it stop.\n\nI’m not washing my hands over and over or checking locks a dozen times, if that’s what you mean. It’s more just… I get bothered by something and then I think about it too much afterward. But compelled to act on it? No, I haven’t noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s probably the thing that’s been bothering me most, if I’m being honest. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it. I’ll be doing something ordinary — sitting at my kitchen table, maybe trying to have my coffee or sort through the mail — and then out of nowhere I hear that man’s rough voice in my head again. Clear as anything, like he’s right there.\n\nOr I’ll suddenly see that donation envelope hitting the ground. Just that moment, over and over, even when I’m trying to think about something else. It’s strange because nothing is happening in the room, it’s quiet, and still my mind just pulls me right back to it. I try to shake it off, tell myself to stop going over it, but it comes in anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do these repeating thoughts cause you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… they do. I mean, I don’t fall apart every time, but it upsets me more than I’d like to admit. It’s the way it comes out of nowhere. I can be sitting at my kitchen table, just minding my own business, and suddenly I hear that man’s rough voice again in my head, or I see that donation envelope hitting the ground like it’s happening right in front of me.\n\nAnd it’s not even that I’m trying to remember it. That’s what bothers me. If I sat down and chose to think about it, maybe that would be one thing, but this just sort of pushes its way in. Then I feel tense and unsettled, and it takes me a while to get back to whatever I was doing. It makes a quiet moment not feel quiet anymore, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did something particularly stressful or traumatic happen to you before these repeating thoughts and loss of temper started?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, there was. It started after I was robbed outside the bank. That was about a week ago now, though it feels a little strange saying “only a week,” because it keeps replaying enough that it feels longer in my head.\n\nBefore that happened, I wasn’t having these repeating thoughts like this, and I wasn’t getting so worked up. But ever since then — since that incident outside the bank — it’s been going on. I keep thinking, well, maybe in another week it’ll fade, but right now it’s still very much there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities since the robbery outside the bank?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have been, though I didn’t quite think of it that way at first. I’ve been taking the long way around now so I don’t have to pass the bank. It’s silly, maybe, because it’s just a building, and I know that, but when I come near it I can feel myself wanting to steer away before I even really decide to.\n\nAnd there’s the festival storage room, too. I haven’t gone in there since. There are things in there I probably should check on, but I keep putting it off because it brings the whole thing too close again. Just being around that sort of donation stuff… it makes me feel like I’m right back there, and I’d rather not put myself through it if I can avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the robbery?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. Not terribly in the sense that I’m up pacing all night, but it’s definitely changed since the robbery.\n\nI’ll get into bed feeling tired — really tired, like my body wants to sleep — and then once the house is quiet, I just sort of lie there listening. Every little noise catches my attention. A creak, a car going by, something settling in the walls… I find myself straining to hear it, as if I’m waiting for something, though I know I’m at home and nothing is happening.\n\nAnd then when I do sleep, it doesn’t feel very deep. I’ve been waking up before dawn, earlier than I need to, and I’ll just be lying there thinking, “Well, that’s it then, I suppose I’m awake.” It leaves me feeling like I hardly slept, even if technically I was in bed for hours.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're in a safe environment?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “constantly,” no. I mean, I understand what you’re asking, but I don’t know that I’m walking around all day scanning for danger exactly.\n\nIt’s more… I’m aware I don’t feel quite like myself, but if you mean am I always looking out the window or checking every corner or expecting someone to come at me, I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way. Maybe I’d have to think about it more, because sometimes you don’t realize you’re doing something until someone points it out. But sitting here now, I can’t honestly say yes, that’s definitely happening.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific reminders of the robbery, such as news reports or conversations about it, that you've been trying to avoid?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have been avoiding certain reminders, though not so much the news reports specifically. It’s more the places and the things connected to it.\n\nI’ve been taking the long way around so I don’t have to pass the bank. I tell myself it’s just more convenient or that I wanted a bit of a walk, but if I’m honest, it’s because going past there makes it feel too close again. Even seeing the front of the building out of the corner of my eye, I feel myself wanting to turn away.\n\nAnd I still haven’t gone into the festival storage room. There are things in there I probably ought to look at, but I keep putting it off. It’s tied up with the whole thing in my mind now, and I don’t like how it brings me right back to it. So yes, I suppose I’ve been avoiding reminders, just maybe in a quieter way than refusing to watch the news or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these repeating thoughts and feelings of distress after the robbery?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a week now, I’d say. Since the robbery outside the bank.\n\nIt started more or less right after that happened — not weeks before or anything like that.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It feels more like something I have to do, if I’m being honest. I wouldn’t call it a preference, exactly. It’s not like I enjoy taking the long way around or like I’ve decided, “Oh, I’d rather not go past the bank today.” It’s more that when I think about passing it, something in me just tightens up, and I start finding reasons not to.

I’ll tell myself the other route is fine, that it only adds a little time, that I needed the walk anyway… but underneath that, I know I’m doing it because I don’t want to see the bank. Even just being near it makes the whole thing feel too close again, like I’m right back at that feeling.

And the festival storage room is similar. I haven’t gone in there because it’s tied to all of it now in my head. I keep thinking I should just open the door and get on with what needs doing, but then I stop myself. So, no, I don’t think it’s just a preference. It feels like I’m avoiding those places because it’s the only way I can keep myself from getting stirred up.

It was such a small thing, and I made it into something bigger. It bothered me enough that I thought maybe I ought to talk to someone before I do it again.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any recurring thoughts or feelings that you feel compelled to repeat or act on?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… not in the way you’re saying, I don’t believe. I mean, I do go back over things in my head sometimes, probably more than I should — like what I said, whether I handled something badly — but I wouldn’t say I feel forced to repeat anything or do some particular action to make it stop.\n\nI’m not washing my hands over and over or checking locks a dozen times, if that’s what you mean. It’s more just… I get bothered by something and then I think about it too much afterward. But compelled to act on it? No, I haven’t noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s probably the thing that’s been bothering me most, if I’m being honest. Clear as anything, like he’s right there.\n\nOr I’ll suddenly see that donation envelope hitting the ground. Just that moment, over and over, even when I’m trying to think about something else. It’s strange because nothing is happening in the room, it’s quiet, and still my mind just pulls me right back to it. I try to shake it off, tell myself to stop going over it, but it comes in anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do these repeating thoughts cause you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… they do. I mean, I don’t fall apart every time, but it upsets me more than I’d like to admit. It’s the way it comes out of nowhere. I can be sitting at my kitchen table, just minding my own business, and suddenly I hear that man’s rough voice again in my head, or I see that donation envelope hitting the ground like it’s happening right in front of me.\n\nAnd it’s not even that I’m trying to remember it. That’s what bothers me. If I sat down and chose to think about it, maybe that would be one thing, but this just sort of pushes its way in. Then I feel tense and unsettled, and it takes me a while to get back to whatever I was doing. It makes a quiet moment not feel quiet anymore, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did something particularly stressful or traumatic happen to you before these repeating thoughts and loss of temper started?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, there was. It started after I was robbed outside the bank. That was about a week ago now, though it feels a little strange saying “only a week,” because it keeps replaying enough that it feels longer in my head.\n\nBefore that happened, I wasn’t having these repeating thoughts like this, and I wasn’t getting so worked up. But ever since then — since that incident outside the bank — it’s been going on. I keep thinking, well, maybe in another week it’ll fade, but right now it’s still very much there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities since the robbery outside the bank?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have been, though I didn’t quite think of it that way at first. I’ve been taking the long way around now so I don’t have to pass the bank. It’s silly, maybe, because it’s just a building, and I know that, but when I come near it I can feel myself wanting to steer away before I even really decide to.\n\nAnd there’s the festival storage room, too. I haven’t gone in there since. There are things in there I probably should check on, but I keep putting it off because it brings the whole thing too close again. Just being around that sort of donation stuff… it makes me feel like I’m right back there, and I’d rather not put myself through it if I can avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the robbery?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. Not terribly in the sense that I’m up pacing all night, but it’s definitely changed since the robbery.\n\nI’ll get into bed feeling tired — really tired, like my body wants to sleep — and then once the house is quiet, I just sort of lie there listening. Every little noise catches my attention. A creak, a car going by, something settling in the walls… I find myself straining to hear it, as if I’m waiting for something, though I know I’m at home and nothing is happening.\n\nAnd then when I do sleep, it doesn’t feel very deep. I’ve been waking up before dawn, earlier than I need to, and I’ll just be lying there thinking, “Well, that’s it then, I suppose I’m awake.” It leaves me feeling like I hardly slept, even if technically I was in bed for hours.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're in a safe environment?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “constantly,” no. I mean, I understand what you’re asking, but I don’t know that I’m walking around all day scanning for danger exactly.\n\nIt’s more… I’m aware I don’t feel quite like myself, but if you mean am I always looking out the window or checking every corner or expecting someone to come at me, I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way. Maybe I’d have to think about it more, because sometimes you don’t realize you’re doing something until someone points it out. Even seeing the front of the building out of the corner of my eye, I feel myself wanting to turn away.\n\nAnd I still haven’t gone into the festival storage room. around so I don't pass the bank, and I haven't gone into the festival storage room because it brings the whole thing too close.\n- key: S071\n  name: Trauma_Related_Amnesia\n  description: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).\n  manifestation: I remember the gun and the envelope on the sidewalk, but the part where I got knocked down is just missing, like someone cut that piece out.\n- key: S077\n  name: Irritability_Aggression\n  description: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.\n  manifestation: What brought me in is that I blew up at one of the younger volunteers over a crooked tablecloth, and I don't usually talk to people that way.\n- key: S080\n  name: Exaggerated_Startle_Response\n  description: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.\n  manifestation: When a car backfired near the senior center, I jumped so hard my chest hurt and my hands kept shaking for a few minutes after.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I haven't been able to keep up with organizing the Día de los Muertos event the way I usually do, and I've stopped going to card nights because I don't feel steady around people right now.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 week\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a week, ever since the robbery outside the bank.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 79 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S077\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these repeating thoughts and feelings of distress after the robbery?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a week now, I’d say. Since the robbery outside the bank.\n\nIt started more or less right after that happened — not weeks before or anything like that. At first I thought maybe it was just the shock of it, you know, the first day or two being rattled, but it hasn’t quite lifted the way I expected. So… yes, about a week.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your avoidance of certain places or activities is interfering with your daily life or routine?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nI usually help keep things moving for the Día de los Muertos event — you know, organizing, checking on what still needs doing, making sure people have what they need. It’s something I’ve done for a long time, and normally I’m pretty dependable with it. But lately I just haven’t been keeping up the way I usually do. I put things off, or I avoid going near certain parts of it, and then the day goes by and I realize I didn’t handle what I said I would.\n\nAnd I’ve stopped going to card nights too. That’s not like me either. It’s not that I don’t care about the people there — I do. But right now I don’t feel steady around people, if that makes sense. I feel like I’d rather stay home than sit there trying to act normal. So yes, I’d say it’s starting to get in the way of my routine. Not in some dramatic way, maybe, but enough that I notice I’m not doing the things I normally do.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that avoiding these places or activities is something you feel like you have to do to cope with your feelings, or is it more of a preference?\n\nPatient: It feels more like something I have to do, if I’m being honest. I wouldn’t call it a preference, exactly. It’s not like I enjoy taking the long way around or like I’ve decided, “Oh, I’d rather not go past the bank today.” It’s more that when I think about passing it, something in me just tightens up, and I start finding reasons not to.\n\nI’ll tell myself the other route is fine, that it only adds a little time, that I needed the walk anyway… but underneath that, I know I’m doing it because I don’t want to see the bank. Even just being near it makes the whole thing feel too close again, like I’m right back at that feeling.\n\nAnd the festival storage room is similar. I haven’t gone in there because it’s tied to all of it now in my head. I keep thinking I should just open the door and get on with what needs doing, but then I stop myself. So, no, I don’t think it’s just a preference. It feels like I’m avoiding those places because it’s the only way I can keep myself from getting stirred up.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that avoiding these places or activities is significantly impacting your ability to function in your daily life?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I’d say it is, though maybe not in a way where everything has fallen apart. I’m still getting through the day, but I’m not functioning like I normally do.

The clearest thing is the Día de los Muertos event. I usually help organize things, and people kind of count on me to keep track of what needs to be done. I’ve done that for years, so it bothers me that I haven’t been keeping up with it the way I usually would. I’ll mean to make a call, or check on something, or go handle something, and then I just… don’t. Or I put it off because it means being around certain places or people, and then I feel bad because someone else has to pick up the slack.

And I’ve stopped going to card nights too, which is a regular part of my week. That may sound small, but at my age those routines matter. It’s where I see people, talk, laugh a little, feel normal. Lately I don’t feel steady enough around people, like I’d have to pretend I’m fine the whole time, and I just don’t have it in me. So I stay home instead.

So yes, it’s affecting my daily life. Mildly maybe, compared to some people, but enough that I notice I’m pulling back from the things I normally do.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well... I suppose what finally pushed me to come in was that I lost my temper in a way that really surprised me. I was helping out with some of the younger volunteers, and there was this tablecloth that was crooked — just a tablecloth, nothing important really — and I snapped at one of them over it. Raised my voice, got sharp with him. That’s not how I usually speak to people, especially young folks who are just trying to help.\n\nAfterward I kept thinking, why did I do that? It was such a small thing, and I made it into something bigger. It bothered me enough that I thought maybe I ought to talk to someone before I do it again.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any recurring thoughts or feelings that you feel compelled to repeat or act on?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… not in the way you’re saying, I don’t believe. I mean, I do go back over things in my head sometimes, probably more than I should — like what I said, whether I handled something badly — but I wouldn’t say I feel forced to repeat anything or do some particular action to make it stop.\n\nI’m not washing my hands over and over or checking locks a dozen times, if that’s what you mean. It’s more just… I get bothered by something and then I think about it too much afterward. But compelled to act on it? No, I haven’t noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s probably the thing that’s been bothering me most, if I’m being honest. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it. I’ll be doing something ordinary — sitting at my kitchen table, maybe trying to have my coffee or sort through the mail — and then out of nowhere I hear that man’s rough voice in my head again. Clear as anything, like he’s right there.\n\nOr I’ll suddenly see that donation envelope hitting the ground. Just that moment, over and over, even when I’m trying to think about something else. It’s strange because nothing is happening in the room, it’s quiet, and still my mind just pulls me right back to it. I try to shake it off, tell myself to stop going over it, but it comes in anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do these repeating thoughts cause you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… they do. I mean, I don’t fall apart every time, but it upsets me more than I’d like to admit. It’s the way it comes out of nowhere. I can be sitting at my kitchen table, just minding my own business, and suddenly I hear that man’s rough voice again in my head, or I see that donation envelope hitting the ground like it’s happening right in front of me.\n\nAnd it’s not even that I’m trying to remember it. That’s what bothers me. If I sat down and chose to think about it, maybe that would be one thing, but this just sort of pushes its way in. Then I feel tense and unsettled, and it takes me a while to get back to whatever I was doing. It makes a quiet moment not feel quiet anymore, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did something particularly stressful or traumatic happen to you before these repeating thoughts and loss of temper started?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, there was. It started after I was robbed outside the bank. That was about a week ago now, though it feels a little strange saying “only a week,” because it keeps replaying enough that it feels longer in my head.\n\nBefore that happened, I wasn’t having these repeating thoughts like this, and I wasn’t getting so worked up. But ever since then — since that incident outside the bank — it’s been going on. I keep thinking, well, maybe in another week it’ll fade, but right now it’s still very much there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities since the robbery outside the bank?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have been, though I didn’t quite think of it that way at first. I’ve been taking the long way around now so I don’t have to pass the bank. It’s silly, maybe, because it’s just a building, and I know that, but when I come near it I can feel myself wanting to steer away before I even really decide to.\n\nAnd there’s the festival storage room, too. I haven’t gone in there since. There are things in there I probably should check on, but I keep putting it off because it brings the whole thing too close again. Just being around that sort of donation stuff… it makes me feel like I’m right back there, and I’d rather not put myself through it if I can avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the robbery?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. Not terribly in the sense that I’m up pacing all night, but it’s definitely changed since the robbery.\n\nI’ll get into bed feeling tired — really tired, like my body wants to sleep — and then once the house is quiet, I just sort of lie there listening. Every little noise catches my attention. A creak, a car going by, something settling in the walls… I find myself straining to hear it, as if I’m waiting for something, though I know I’m at home and nothing is happening.\n\nAnd then when I do sleep, it doesn’t feel very deep. I’ve been waking up before dawn, earlier than I need to, and I’ll just be lying there thinking, “Well, that’s it then, I suppose I’m awake.” It leaves me feeling like I hardly slept, even if technically I was in bed for hours.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're in a safe environment?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “constantly,” no. I mean, I understand what you’re asking, but I don’t know that I’m walking around all day scanning for danger exactly.\n\nIt’s more… I’m aware I don’t feel quite like myself, but if you mean am I always looking out the window or checking every corner or expecting someone to come at me, I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way. Maybe I’d have to think about it more, because sometimes you don’t realize you’re doing something until someone points it out. But sitting here now, I can’t honestly say yes, that’s definitely happening.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific reminders of the robbery, such as news reports or conversations about it, that you've been trying to avoid?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have been avoiding certain reminders, though not so much the news reports specifically. It’s more the places and the things connected to it.\n\nI’ve been taking the long way around so I don’t have to pass the bank. I tell myself it’s just more convenient or that I wanted a bit of a walk, but if I’m honest, it’s because going past there makes it feel too close again. Even seeing the front of the building out of the corner of my eye, I feel myself wanting to turn away.\n\nAnd I still haven’t gone into the festival storage room. There are things in there I probably ought to look at, but I keep putting it off. It’s tied up with the whole thing in my mind now, and I don’t like how it brings me right back to it. So yes, I suppose I’ve been avoiding reminders, just maybe in a quieter way than refusing to watch the news or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these repeating thoughts and feelings of distress after the robbery?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a week now, I’d say. Since the robbery outside the bank.\n\nIt started more or less right after that happened — not weeks before or anything like that. At first I thought maybe it was just the shock of it, you know, the first day or two being rattled, but it hasn’t quite lifted the way I expected. So… yes, about a week.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your avoidance of certain places or activities is interfering with your daily life or routine?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nI usually help keep things moving for the Día de los Muertos event — you know, organizing, checking on what still needs doing, making sure people have what they need. It’s something I’ve done for a long time, and normally I’m pretty dependable with it. But lately I just haven’t been keeping up the way I usually do. I put things off, or I avoid going near certain parts of it, and then the day goes by and I realize I didn’t handle what I said I would.\n\nAnd I’ve stopped going to card nights too. That’s not like me either. It’s not that I don’t care about the people there — I do. But right now I don’t feel steady around people, if that makes sense. I feel like I’d rather stay home than sit there trying to act normal. So yes, I’d say it’s starting to get in the way of my routine. Just being around that sort of donation stuff… it makes me feel like I’m right back there, and I’d rather not put myself through it if I can avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the robbery?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. Not terribly in the sense that I’m up pacing all night, but it’s definitely changed since the robbery.\n\nI’ll get into bed feeling tired — really tired, like my body wants to sleep — and then once the house is quiet, I just sort of lie there listening. Every little noise catches my attention. A creak, a car going by, something settling in the walls… I find myself straining to hear it, as if I’m waiting for something, though I know I’m at home and nothing is happening.\n\nAnd then when I do sleep, it doesn’t feel very deep. I’ve been waking up before dawn, earlier than I need to, and I’ll just be lying there thinking, “Well, that’s it then, I suppose I’m awake.” It leaves me feeling like I hardly slept, even if technically I was in bed for hours.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're in a safe environment?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “constantly,” no. I mean, I understand what you’re asking, but I don’t know that I’m walking around all day scanning for danger exactly.\n\nIt’s more… I’m aware I don’t feel quite like myself, but if you mean am I always looking out the window or checking every corner or expecting someone to come at me, I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way. Maybe I’d have to think about it more, because sometimes you don’t realize you’re doing something until someone points it out. But sitting here now, I can’t honestly say yes, that’s definitely happening.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific reminders of the robbery, such as news reports or conversations about it, that you've been trying to avoid?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have been avoiding certain reminders, though not so much the news reports specifically. It’s more the places and the things connected to it.\n\nI’ve been taking the long way around so I don’t have to pass the bank. I tell myself it’s just more convenient or that I wanted a bit of a walk, but if I’m honest, it’s because going past there makes it feel too close again. Even seeing the front of the building out of the corner of my eye, I feel myself wanting to turn away.\n\nAnd I still haven’t gone into the festival storage room. There are things in there I probably ought to look at, but I keep putting it off. It’s tied up with the whole thing in my mind now, and I don’t like how it brings me right back to it. So yes, I suppose I’ve been avoiding reminders, just maybe in a quieter way than refusing to watch the news or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these repeating thoughts and feelings of distress after the robbery?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a week now, I’d say. Since the robbery outside the bank.\n\nIt started more or less right after that happened — not weeks before or anything like that. At first I thought maybe it was just the shock of it, you know, the first day or two being rattled, but it hasn’t quite lifted the way I expected. So… yes, about a week.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your avoidance of certain places or activities is interfering with your daily life or routine?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nI usually help keep things moving for the Día de los Muertos event — you know, organizing, checking on what still needs doing, making sure people have what they need. It’s something I’ve done for a long time, and normally I’m pretty dependable with it. But lately I just haven’t been keeping up the way I usually do. I put things off, or I avoid going near certain parts of it, and then the day goes by and I realize I didn’t handle what I said I would.\n\nAnd I’ve stopped going to card nights too. That’s not like me either. It’s not that I don’t care about the people there — I do. But right now I don’t feel steady around people, if that makes sense. I feel like I’d rather stay home than sit there trying to act normal. So yes, I’d say it’s starting to get in the way of my routine. Not in some dramatic way, maybe, but enough that I notice I’m not doing the things I normally do.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that avoiding these places or activities is something you feel like you have to do to cope with your feelings, or is it more of a preference?\n\nPatient: It feels more like something I have to do, if I’m being honest. I wouldn’t call it a preference, exactly. It’s not like I enjoy taking the long way around or like I’ve decided, “Oh, I’d rather not go past the bank today.” It’s more that when I think about passing it, something in me just tightens up, and I start finding reasons not to.\n\nI’ll tell myself the other route is fine, that it only adds a little time, that I needed the walk anyway… but underneath that, I know I’m doing it because I don’t want to see the bank. Even just being near it makes the whole thing feel too close again, like I’m right back at that feeling.\n\nAnd the festival storage room is similar. I haven’t gone in there because it’s tied to all of it now in my head. I keep thinking I should just open the door and get on with what needs doing, but then I stop myself. So, no, I don’t think it’s just a preference. It feels like I’m avoiding those places because it’s the only way I can keep myself from getting stirred up.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that avoiding these places or activities is significantly impacting your ability to function in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’d say it is, though maybe not in a way where everything has fallen apart. I’m still getting through the day, but I’m not functioning like I normally do.\n\nThe clearest thing is the Día de los Muertos event. I usually help organize things, and people kind of count on me to keep track of what needs to be done. I’ve done that for years, so it bothers me that I haven’t been keeping up with it the way I usually would. I’ll mean to make a call, or check on something, or go handle something, and then I just… don’t. Or I put it off because it means being around certain places or people, and then I feel bad because someone else has to pick up the slack.\n\nAnd I’ve stopped going to card nights too, which is a regular part of my week. That may sound small, but at my age those routines matter. It’s where I see people, talk, laugh a little, feel normal. Lately I don’t feel steady enough around people, like I’d have to pretend I’m fine the whole time, and I just don’t have it in me. So I stay home instead.\n\nSo yes, it’s affecting my daily life. Mildly maybe, compared to some people, but enough that I notice I’m pulling back from the things I normally do.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’d say it’s more than occasional, yes. Not every single minute of the day, but it’s become sort of… built into how I’m moving around now.

Like with the bank, I’ve been taking the long way around so I don’t have to pass it. At first I told myself it was just once or twice, just because I didn’t feel up to it that day. But then I noticed I was doing it again, and again, almost without even deciding. I’ll be walking or driving and before I know it, I’ve already planned a route that keeps me away from there. It’s not like I sit down and make a big dramatic decision about it — it’s more automatic than that. I just don’t want to see it, don’t want to get that feeling in my chest, so I go around.

And with the festival storage room, I haven’t gone in there at all. I know there are probably things in there I should be checking on, and normally I would’ve just gone in, looked over the supplies, made a list, done what needed doing. But now even thinking about opening that door makes the whole thing feel too close again. So I keep putting it off.

So… I guess I’d say it’s happening most of the time when those places come up. It’s not that I’m avoiding everything in life, but those specific places, yes — I keep steering away from them.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well... I suppose what finally pushed me to come in was that I lost my temper in a way that really surprised me. I was helping out with some of the younger volunteers, and there was this tablecloth that was crooked — just a tablecloth, nothing important really — and I snapped at one of them over it. Raised my voice, got sharp with him. That’s not how I usually speak to people, especially young folks who are just trying to help.\n\nAfterward I kept thinking, why did I do that? It was such a small thing, and I made it into something bigger. It bothered me enough that I thought maybe I ought to talk to someone before I do it again.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any recurring thoughts or feelings that you feel compelled to repeat or act on?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… not in the way you’re saying, I don’t believe. I mean, I do go back over things in my head sometimes, probably more than I should — like what I said, whether I handled something badly — but I wouldn’t say I feel forced to repeat anything or do some particular action to make it stop.\n\nI’m not washing my hands over and over or checking locks a dozen times, if that’s what you mean. It’s more just… I get bothered by something and then I think about it too much afterward. But compelled to act on it? No, I haven’t noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s probably the thing that’s been bothering me most, if I’m being honest. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it. I’ll be doing something ordinary — sitting at my kitchen table, maybe trying to have my coffee or sort through the mail — and then out of nowhere I hear that man’s rough voice in my head again. Clear as anything, like he’s right there.\n\nOr I’ll suddenly see that donation envelope hitting the ground. Just that moment, over and over, even when I’m trying to think about something else. It’s strange because nothing is happening in the room, it’s quiet, and still my mind just pulls me right back to it. I try to shake it off, tell myself to stop going over it, but it comes in anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do these repeating thoughts cause you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… they do. I mean, I don’t fall apart every time, but it upsets me more than I’d like to admit. It’s the way it comes out of nowhere. I can be sitting at my kitchen table, just minding my own business, and suddenly I hear that man’s rough voice again in my head, or I see that donation envelope hitting the ground like it’s happening right in front of me.\n\nAnd it’s not even that I’m trying to remember it. That’s what bothers me. If I sat down and chose to think about it, maybe that would be one thing, but this just sort of pushes its way in. Then I feel tense and unsettled, and it takes me a while to get back to whatever I was doing. It makes a quiet moment not feel quiet anymore, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did something particularly stressful or traumatic happen to you before these repeating thoughts and loss of temper started?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, there was. It started after I was robbed outside the bank. That was about a week ago now, though it feels a little strange saying “only a week,” because it keeps replaying enough that it feels longer in my head.\n\nBefore that happened, I wasn’t having these repeating thoughts like this, and I wasn’t getting so worked up. But ever since then — since that incident outside the bank — it’s been going on. I keep thinking, well, maybe in another week it’ll fade, but right now it’s still very much there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities since the robbery outside the bank?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have been, though I didn’t quite think of it that way at first. I’ve been taking the long way around now so I don’t have to pass the bank. It’s silly, maybe, because it’s just a building, and I know that, but when I come near it I can feel myself wanting to steer away before I even really decide to.\n\nAnd there’s the festival storage room, too. I haven’t gone in there since. There are things in there I probably should check on, but I keep putting it off because it brings the whole thing too close again. Just being around that sort of donation stuff… it makes me feel like I’m right back there, and I’d rather not put myself through it if I can avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the robbery?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. Not terribly in the sense that I’m up pacing all night, but it’s definitely changed since the robbery.\n\nI’ll get into bed feeling tired — really tired, like my body wants to sleep — and then once the house is quiet, I just sort of lie there listening. Every little noise catches my attention. A creak, a car going by, something settling in the walls… I find myself straining to hear it, as if I’m waiting for something, though I know I’m at home and nothing is happening.\n\nAnd then when I do sleep, it doesn’t feel very deep. I’ve been waking up before dawn, earlier than I need to, and I’ll just be lying there thinking, “Well, that’s it then, I suppose I’m awake.” It leaves me feeling like I hardly slept, even if technically I was in bed for hours.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're in a safe environment?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “constantly,” no. I mean, I understand what you’re asking, but I don’t know that I’m walking around all day scanning for danger exactly.\n\nIt’s more… I’m aware I don’t feel quite like myself, but if you mean am I always looking out the window or checking every corner or expecting someone to come at me, I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way. Maybe I’d have to think about it more, because sometimes you don’t realize you’re doing something until someone points it out. But sitting here now, I can’t honestly say yes, that’s definitely happening.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific reminders of the robbery, such as news reports or conversations about it, that you've been trying to avoid?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have been avoiding certain reminders, though not so much the news reports specifically. It’s more the places and the things connected to it.\n\nI’ve been taking the long way around so I don’t have to pass the bank. I tell myself it’s just more convenient or that I wanted a bit of a walk, but if I’m honest, it’s because going past there makes it feel too close again. Even seeing the front of the building out of the corner of my eye, I feel myself wanting to turn away.\n\nAnd I still haven’t gone into the festival storage room. There are things in there I probably ought to look at, but I keep putting it off. It’s tied up with the whole thing in my mind now, and I don’t like how it brings me right back to it. So yes, I suppose I’ve been avoiding reminders, just maybe in a quieter way than refusing to watch the news or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these repeating thoughts and feelings of distress after the robbery?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a week now, I’d say. Since the robbery outside the bank.\n\nIt started more or less right after that happened — not weeks before or anything like that. At first I thought maybe it was just the shock of it, you know, the first day or two being rattled, but it hasn’t quite lifted the way I expected. So… yes, about a week.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your avoidance of certain places or activities is interfering with your daily life or routine?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nI usually help keep things moving for the Día de los Muertos event — you know, organizing, checking on what still needs doing, making sure people have what they need. It’s something I’ve done for a long time, and normally I’m pretty dependable with it. But lately I just haven’t been keeping up the way I usually do. I put things off, or I avoid going near certain parts of it, and then the day goes by and I realize I didn’t handle what I said I would.\n\nAnd I’ve stopped going to card nights too. That’s not like me either. It’s not that I don’t care about the people there — I do. But right now I don’t feel steady around people, if that makes sense. I feel like I’d rather stay home than sit there trying to act normal. So yes, I’d say it’s starting to get in the way of my routine. Not in some dramatic way, maybe, but enough that I notice I’m not doing the things I normally do.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that avoiding these places or activities is something you feel like you have to do to cope with your feelings, or is it more of a preference?\n\nPatient: It feels more like something I have to do, if I’m being honest. I wouldn’t call it a preference, exactly. It’s not like I enjoy taking the long way around or like I’ve decided, “Oh, I’d rather not go past the bank today.” It’s more that when I think about passing it, something in me just tightens up, and I start finding reasons not to.\n\nI’ll tell myself the other route is fine, that it only adds a little time, that I needed the walk anyway… but underneath that, I know I’m doing it because I don’t want to see the bank. Even just being near it makes the whole thing feel too close again, like I’m right back at that feeling.\n\nAnd the festival storage room is similar. I haven’t gone in there because it’s tied to all of it now in my head. I keep thinking I should just open the door and get on with what needs doing, but then I stop myself. So, no, I don’t think it’s just a preference. It feels like I’m avoiding those places because it’s the only way I can keep myself from getting stirred up.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that avoiding these places or activities is significantly impacting your ability to function in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’d say it is, though maybe not in a way where everything has fallen apart. I’m still getting through the day, but I’m not functioning like I normally do.\n\nThe clearest thing is the Día de los Muertos event. I usually help organize things, and people kind of count on me to keep track of what needs to be done. I’ve done that for years, so it bothers me that I haven’t been keeping up with it the way I usually would. I’ll mean to make a call, or check on something, or go handle something, and then I just… don’t. Or I put it off because it means being around certain places or people, and then I feel bad because someone else has to pick up the slack.\n\nAnd I’ve stopped going to card nights too, which is a regular part of my week. That may sound small, but at my age those routines matter. It’s where I see people, talk, laugh a little, feel normal. Lately I don’t feel steady enough around people, like I’d have to pretend I’m fine the whole time, and I just don’t have it in me. So I stay home instead.\n\nSo yes, it’s affecting my daily life. Mildly maybe, compared to some people, but enough that I notice I’m pulling back from the things I normally do.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that avoiding these places or activities is something you feel like you're doing most of the time, or is it more occasional?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more than occasional, yes. Not every single minute of the day, but it’s become sort of… built into how I’m moving around now.\n\nLike with the bank, I’ve been taking the long way around so I don’t have to pass it. At first I told myself it was just once or twice, just because I didn’t feel up to it that day. But then I noticed I was doing it again, and again, almost without even deciding. I’ll be walking or driving and before I know it, I’ve already planned a route that keeps me away from there. It’s not like I sit down and make a big dramatic decision about it — it’s more automatic than that. I just don’t want to see it, don’t want to get that feeling in my chest, so I go around.\n\nAnd with the festival storage room, I haven’t gone in there at all. I know there are probably things in there I should be checking on, and normally I would’ve just gone in, looked over the supplies, made a list, done what needed doing. But now even thinking about opening that door makes the whole thing feel too close again. So I keep putting it off.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say it’s happening most of the time when those places come up. It’s not that I’m avoiding everything in life, but those specific places, yes — I keep steering away from them.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
